This paper is a continuation of a previous work by two of the Authors [ 36 ] on long time existence for Boussinesq systems modeling the propagation of long, weakly nonlinear water waves. We provide proofs on examples not considered in [ 36 ] in particular we prove a long time well-posedness result for a delicate "strongly dispersive" Boussinesq system. 1. Introduction One aim of this paper is to complete the results obtained in a previous paper [ 36 ] on the Cauchy theory for some (a,b,c,d) Boussinesq systems for surface water waves (1.1) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})+\mu[a\nabla\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}-b\Delta\eta_{t}]=0\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\epsilon\frac{1}{2}\nabla|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\mu[c\nabla\Delta\eta-d\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}]=0.\end{array}\right.$ Here $\mu$ and $\epsilon$ are the small parameters (shallowness and nonlinearity parameters respectively) defined as $\mu=\frac{h^{2}}{\lambda^{2}},\quad\epsilon=\frac{\alpha}{h}$ where $\alpha$ is a typical amplitude of the wave, $h$ a typical depth and $\lambda$ a typical horizontal wavelength. In the Boussinesq regime, $\epsilon$ and $\mu$ are supposed to be of same order, $\epsilon\sim\mu\ll 1,$ and we will take for simplicity $\epsilon=\mu,$ writing ( 1.1 ) as (1.2) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon[\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})+a\nabla\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}-b\Delta\eta_{t}]=0\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\epsilon[\frac{1}{2}\nabla|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+c\nabla\Delta\eta-d\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}]=0,\end{array}\right.$ The class of systems ( 1.1 ), ( 1.2 ) models water waves on a flat bottom propagating in both directions in the aforementioned regime (see [ 6 , 7 , 5 ] ). We will focus here on the strongly dispersive case, corresponding to particular choices of the modeling parameters (a,b,c,d) (see below). One could also derive similar systems with a non trivial bathymetry (non flat bottom), see [ 13 ] , and one has then to distinguish between the case when the bottom varies slowly and the case where it is strongly varying. In the former case, ( 1.2 ) has to be slightly modified and becomes (1.3) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon[\nabla\cdot((\eta-\beta){\boldsymbol{u}})+a\nabla\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}-b\Delta\eta_{t}]=0\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\epsilon[\frac{1}{2}\nabla|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+c\nabla\Delta\eta-d\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}]=0,\end{array}\right.$ where $\beta$ is a smooth function on $\mathbb{R}^{d},d=1,2,$ bounded together with its derivatives. In this case, the results in [ 36 ] and those of the present paper extend easily.
In the second case one gets much more complicated systems [ 13 ] . We refer to [ 32 ] for long time existence results in this case. Recall (see [ 6 , 15 ] ) that the modeling parameters are constrained by the relation $a+b+c+d=\frac{1}{3}-\tau,$ where $\tau\geq 0$ is the surface tension parameter (Bond number). Recall also [ 6 ] that ( 1.2 ) is linearly well-posed when $a\leq 0,c\leq 0,b\geq 0,d\geq 0,$ and when $a=c,b\geq 0,d\geq 0.$ An important step to justify rigorously ( 1.2 ) as an asymptotic model for water waves is to establish the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem on time scales of order $1/\epsilon,$ with uniform bounds in suitable Sobolev spaces, the error estimate being then (see [ 5 , 29 ] ). $||U_{\text{Boussinesq}}-U_{\text{Euler}}||=O(\epsilon^{2}t)$ in suitable Sobolev norms. This step has been established in [ 36 ] (see also [ 34 ] ) for most of Boussinesq systems with and without surface tension. The idea in [ 36 ] is to find an appropriate symmetrization of the system and this is not a straightforward task since one cannot obviously use the classical symmetrizer of the underlying Saint-Venant (shallow water) hyperbolic system. This will be reviewed in the first section of this paper. A complete proof of cases that were not fully developed in [ 36 ] will be given in Section 4. 1 1 1 Due to the large number of cases to be considered, we chosed in [ 36 ] to give complete proofs for a limited number of cases. Introducing surface tension enlarges the range of physically admissible parameters (a,b,c,d) and so even a local theory 2 2 2 That is not taking care of the dependence of the lifespan of the solution with respect to $\epsilon$ for a few linearly well -posed systems is still missing, for instance the cases $b=d=0,a<0,c=0$ and $b=d=0,a=0,c<0$ ). Both cases will be considered here but the later leads to serious difficulties and the long time existence for it is the main result of the present paper. Note also that the (linearly well-posed) "exceptional KdV-KdV" case $b=d=0,a=c>0$ which is studied in [ 30 ] leading to well-posedness on time scales of order $1/\sqrt{\epsilon}$ in Sobolev spaces $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),s>3/2$ which are larger than the "hyperbolic" one $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),s>2$ is not covered neither in [ 36 ] nor in the present paper so that a long time existence is still open in this case 3 3 3 However, the case $b=d=0,a<0,c<0$ that can only occur with a strong surface tension is covered by the theory in [ 36 ] . . An important mathematical issue concerning Boussinesq systems ( 1.2 ) is that despite they describe the same dynamics of water waves, their mathematical properties are rather different, due essentially to their different linear dispersion relations. Of course those dispersion relations all coincide in the long wave limit but there are quite different in the short wave limit. A convenient way to classify the system is according to the order of the Fourier multiplier operator given by the eigenvalues of the linearized operator (see [ 6 ] ). The order can be $-1,0,1,2$ or $3$ . The two last cases are referred to as the strongly dispersive ones. After a brief review of our previous results, we will consider in the third section the ("local") Cauchy problem for two strongly dispersive Boussinesq systems of Schrödinger type , namely $b=d=c=0,a<0$ and $a=b=d=0,c<0,$ two situations that are admissible in case of strong surface tension and that have not been considered before. In the first case, it turns out that the local (that is on time scales of order $1/\sqrt{\epsilon})$ Cauchy theory can be obtained by "elementary" energy methods on the original formulation, as in the purely gravity waves cases $a<0,c<0,b=0,d>0$ or $a<0,c<0,b>0,d=0$ considered in [ 30 ] . On the other hand, the second case $a=b=d=0,c<0,$ leads to serious difficulties that are explained in this section. In the fourth section we provide detailed proofs (not given in [ 36 ] ) for the long time well-posedness of the strongly dispersive case $b=d=c=0,a<0$ and for two systems which can be viewed as weakly dispersive , namely $b>0,a=c=d=0$ and $d>0,a=b=c=0.$ We conclude this section by establishing long time existence for the difficult case $a=b=d=0,c<0$ by a quasilinearization method quite different from the other cases. As was aforementioned this is the main result of the present paper (see Theorems 4.6 and 4.7). We explain first how to get the needed a priori estimates, the complete proof being given in the next section. Finally we show in Section 6 that the symmetrization method can be used to obtain long time existence results for a fifth order Boussinesq system and we briefly allude to possible extensions to nonlocal Full dispersion Boussinesq type systems. During the completion of the present paper we were informed of the very interesting paper [ 11 ] where an alternative proof of long time existence for
most of the Boussinesq systems is provided (excluding the "strongly dispersive" ones $b=d=0,$ thus the "difficult case" $a=b=d=0,c<0$ ). This proof also relaxes the non-cavitation condition on the initial data $\eta_{0}.$ We were also informed by Vincent Duchêne of the article [ 18 ] which contains in the one-dimensional case (see Appendix A) results related to ours in Subsection 4.4. Notations. We will denote $|\cdot|_{p}$ the norm in the Lebesgue space $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),\;1\leq p\leq\infty$ and $\|\cdot\|_{s}$ the norm in the Sobolev space $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;s\in\mathbb{R}.$ $(\cdot|\cdot)_{2}$ denotes the scalar product in $L^{2}.$ We will denote $\hat{f}$ or $\mathcal{F}(f)$ the Fourier transform of a tempered distribution $f.$ For any $s\in\mathbb{R},$ we define $|D|^{s}f$ by its Fourier transform $\widehat{|D|^{s}f}(\xi)=|\xi|^{s}\hat{f}(\xi).$ We also denote $|D_{x}|^{s}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(|\xi_{1}|\hat{f})$ and $|D_{y}|^{s}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(|\xi_{2}|\hat{f}).$ Finally we will denote $\Lambda=(I-\Delta)^{1/2}$ and $J_{\epsilon}=(I-\epsilon\Delta)^{1/2}.$ 2. A review of long time well-posed Boussinesq systems As recalled previously, in order to fully justify the Boussinesq systems, one needs to prove the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem on time scales of order at least $O(1/\epsilon)$ (together with the relevant uniform bounds). This would be achieved of course if one could obtain the global well-posedness (also with uniform bounds). This is only known however for a very limited number of Boussinesq systems in one-dimension. A first idea would be to use appropriate conservation laws, but contrary to the one-directional or quasi one-directional equations such as the Korteweg- de Vries or the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili equations which are derived in the same regime, the Boussinesq systems do not possess the two invariants ( $L^{2}$ norm and energy) that provide useful a priori bounds. Nevertheless, when $b=d,$ the Boussinesq systems are endowed with an Hamiltonian structure. More precisely, denoting by $J$ the skew adjoint matrix operator $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{x}(I-\epsilon b\Delta)^{-1}&\partial_{y}(I-\epsilon b\Delta)^{-1}\\
\partial_{x}(I-\epsilon b\Delta)^{-1}&0&0\\
\partial_{y}(I-\epsilon b\Delta)^{-1}&0&0\end{pmatrix},$ and $U=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{pmatrix},$ the Boussinesq systems write in this case $\partial_{t}U=-J(\text{grad}\,H_{\epsilon})(U),$ where $H_{\epsilon}(U)$ is the Hamiltonian given by $H_{\epsilon}(U)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(-c\epsilon|\nabla\eta|^{2}-a\epsilon|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\eta^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\epsilon\eta|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}\big)dxdy,$ so that $H_{\epsilon}(U)$ is conserved by the flow. This can be used (see [ 7 ] ) in the one dimensional case where $b=d>0,a\leq 0,c\leq 0$ or $b=d>0,a=0,c<0$ to establish the global well-posedness of the corresponding Boussinesq systems provided $H_{\epsilon}(\eta_{0},u_{0})$ is small enough and the non cavitation condition $\inf_{x}(1+\epsilon\eta_{0}(x))>0$ is satisfied. The proof uses in a crucial way the fact that $b=d>0$ and the Sobolev embedding $H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ thus it does not work in two dimensions. Another one-dimensional situation leading to global well-posedneess is when $a=b=c=0,d>0.$ Then Amick and Schonbeck [ 4 , 38 ] use the underlying hyperbolic structure of the shallow-water (Saint-Venant) system to get a priori bounds stemming from an entropy functional. This allows to prove the global well-posedness under the condition $\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(1+\epsilon\eta_{0}(x))>0$ 4 4 4 Contrary to what was claimed in [ 36 ] , the results in [ 4 , 38 ] do not need a smallness assumption on the initial data. but again the extension of this result to the two-dimensional case is unclear. We will prove in this case the large time existence in Section 4. As far as long time results are concerned, it has been claimed in [ 36 ] that the Boussinesq systems ( 1.2 ) are well-posed in a suitable Sobolev setting (with uniform bounds) on time scales of order $1/\epsilon$ in the following cases: (1) $b>0,d=0,a,c<0$ ; (2) $b>0,d=0,a=0,c<0$ ; (3) $b=0,d>0,a,c<0$ ; (4) $b\neq d$ , $b,d>0,a,c<0$ or $b=0,d>0,a=0,c<0$ ; (5) $b\neq d,b,d>0,a=0,c<0$ ; (6) $b=d>0,a,c<0$ or $b>0,d=0,a<0,c=0$ ; (7) $b>0,d=0,a=c=0$ or $b=d>0,a=0,c<0$ ; (8) $b,d>0,a<0,c=0$ or $b=0,d>0,a=c=0$ ; (9) $b=0,d>0,a<0,c=0$ ; (10) $b,d>0,a=c=0$ ; (11) $b=d=0,a,c<0$ . Note that the last case can occur only in case of a strong surface tension, as the two following that were not considered in [ 36 ] : (12) $b=d=0,a=0,c<0$ ; (13) $b=d=0,a<0,c=0$ . Actually, the same scheme of proof (by symmetrization) is used in [ 36 ] but because of the many different cases to be dealt with (the technical details cannot be treated in an unified way), we only provided a complete proof in [ 36 ] for cases (4) ("generic case"), (1) and (11), which are "strongly dispersive". The other cases can be dealt with by similar symmetrization techniques but the proofs for some of them need more explanations that we detail below. 3. Some strongly dispersive Boussinesq systems We will study here the local well-posedness (that is on time scales of order $1/\sqrt{\epsilon}$ ) of the Cauchy problem for two strongly dispersive Boussinesq systems having an order two dispersion. They occur only for capillary-gravity waves when the surface tension parameter is greater than $1/3.$ Two (purely gravity waves ) systems having also an order two dispersion corresponding respectively to $a<0,c<0,b=0,d>0$ and $a<0,c<0,d=0,b>0$ have been studied in [ 30 ] under a curl free condition in the later case. The local well-posedness on time scale of order $1/\sqrt{\epsilon}$ was proven there while well-posedness on time scales of order $1/\epsilon$ is established in [ 36 ] together with the appropriate uniform bounds. As in [ 30 ] we will use somehow the dispersive properties of the systems which allows to enlarge the space of resolution but will not provide existence on the "long" time scale $1/\epsilon$ which will be considered in Section 4. Those systems will be referred to as "Schrödinger type" since in space dimension two their dispersion relations for large frequencies are reminiscent of the Schrödinger one (in one dimension, the analogy is with the Benjamin-Ono equation). This will be made clear when rewriting the systems in an equivalent form after diagonalizing the linear part. 3.1. A first Schrödinger type system We consider the Boussinesq systems when $a<0,b=c=d=0,$ a case which occurs for capillary surface waves with strong enough surface tension, $\tau>1/3$ and which was not considered in [ 36 ] . One can obviously restrict to the case where $a=-1$ and we consider first the one-dimensional system (3.1) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+u_{x}+\epsilon(u\eta)_{x}-\epsilon u_{xxx}=0,\\
u_{t}+\eta_{x}+\epsilon uu_{x}=0.\end{array}\right.$ Note that this system has the hamiltonian structure $\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
u\end{pmatrix}+J\text{grad}\;H_{\epsilon}(\eta,u)=0$ where $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $H_{\epsilon}(\eta,u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\epsilon u_{x}^{2}+\eta^{2}+u^{2}+\epsilon u^{2}\eta)dx.$ Unfortunately the formal conservation of the Hamiltonian cannot be used to get a global $L^{2}\times H^{1}$ bound. As for other order two Boussinesq systems (see [ 7 , 30 ] ) one can solve the local Cauchy problem for ( 3.1 ) by "elementary" energy methods. For $U=(\eta,u)^{T}$ we define $||U||_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\eta^{2}+u^{2}+|D^{s}_{x}\eta|^{2}+|D^{s}_{x}u|^{2}+\epsilon|D^{s+1}_{x}u|^{2})dx\right)^{1/2}.$ Theorem 3.1 . Let $s>1/2$ and $(\eta_{0},u_{0})\in X_{\epsilon}^{s}.$ There exists $T_{\epsilon}=O(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})$ and a unique solution $(\eta,u)\in C([0,T_{\epsilon}]);X_{\epsilon}^{s})$ of ( 3.1 ) with initial data $(\eta_{0},u_{0}).$ Proof. In order to restrict the technicalities, we consider only the case $s=1$ and we derive only the suitable a priori estimates. The complete proof would use various Kato-Ponce type commutator estimates and an approximation argument. 5 5 5 We will treat the general situation in the two-dimensional case. We take successively the $L^{2}$ scalar product of the first equation in ( 3.1 ) by $\eta-\eta_{xx}$ and of the second equation by $(I-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})(u-u_{xx}).$ After several integrations by parts we obtain by adding the resulting equations : (3.2) $\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\eta^{2}&+u^{2}+\eta_{x}^{2}+(1+\epsilon)u_{x}^{2}+\epsilon u^{2}_{xx})dx\\
&=-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\frac{1}{2}u_{x}\eta^{2}+\frac{3}{2}u_{x}\eta_{x}^{2}+\eta\eta_{x}u_{xx}+\frac{1+\epsilon}{2}u_{x}^{3}+\frac{5\epsilon}{2}u_{x}u_{xx}^{2}]dx.\end{split}$ We now use Hölder inequality, the standard inequality $|u|_{\infty}\lesssim|u|_{2}^{1/2}|u_{x}|_{2}^{1/2}$ and that $\displaystyle|\eta|_{\infty},\ |\eta|_{2},|\eta_{x}|_{2},|u_{x}|_{2},\sqrt{\epsilon}|u_{x}|_{\infty},\sqrt{\epsilon}|u_{xx}|_{2}\lesssim||U||_{X^{1}_{\epsilon}}$ to obtain from ( 3.2 ) that $||U||_{X_{\epsilon}^{1}}\leq C$ on the maximal existence time interval $[0,T_{\epsilon}]$ of the ODE $\displaystyle y^{\prime}\leq C\sqrt{\epsilon}\;y^{2}$ and one readily checks that $T_{\epsilon}=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}).$ This leads to the existence of a weak solution $U\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{\epsilon};X^{1}_{\epsilon})$ (with an uniform $H^{1}$ bound). To prove uniqueness, we set $N=\eta_{1}-\eta_{2},\;V=u_{1}-u_{2}$ where $(\eta_{1},u_{1}),\;(\eta_{2},u_{2})$ are two solutions in $C([0,T_{\epsilon}]);X_{\epsilon}^{s})$ . Thus (3.3) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}N_{t}+V_{x}+\epsilon[(V\eta_{1})_{x}+(u_{2}N)_{x}]-\epsilon V_{xxx}=0,\\
V_{t}+N_{x}+\epsilon[Vu_{1x}+u_{2}V_{x}]=0.\end{array}\right.$ One takes the $L^{2}$ product scalar of the first equation by $N$ and successively the $L^{2}$ scalar product of the second equation by $V$ and $-\epsilon V_{xx}.$ Adding the resulting equalities we obtain (3.4) $\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(N^{2}+V^{2}+\epsilon V_{x}^{2})dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\{-\epsilon[(V\eta_{1})_{x}N+(u_{2}N)_{x}N]-\epsilon[V^{2}u_{1x}+u_{2}VV_{x}]\\
&\quad+\epsilon^{2}(VV_{xx}u_{1x}+u_{2}V_{x}V_{xx})\}dx,\end{split}$ from which we deduce (3.5) $\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(N^{2}+V^{2}+\epsilon V_{x}^{2})dx\\
&\leq C\epsilon[|\eta_{1}|_{\infty}|V_{x}|_{2}|N|_{2}+|V|_{2}^{1/2}|V_{x}|_{2}^{1/2}|N|_{2}|\eta_{1x}|_{2}\\
&\quad+|u_{2x}|_{\infty}|N|^{2}_{2}+|u_{1x}|_{\infty}|V|^{2}_{2}+|u_{2x}|_{\infty}|V|_{2}^{2}\\
&\quad+\epsilon((|u_{1x}|_{\infty}+|u_{2x}|_{\infty})|V_{x}|_{2}^{2})+|V|_{2}^{1/2}|V_{x}|_{2}^{3/2}|u_{1xx}|_{2})],\end{split}$ so that (3.6) $\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(N^{2}+V^{2}+\epsilon V_{x}^{2})dx\leq C(|N|^{2}_{2}+|V|_{2}^{2}+\epsilon|V_{x}|_{2}^{2})$ and $N=V=0$ by Gronwall’s lemma. It remains to prove the strong continuity in time of the solution with value in $X_{\epsilon}^{1}$ and the continuity of the flow, but this results from the Bona-Smith approximation procedure [ 9 ] .
∎ The local well-posedness of the Cauchy problem ( 3.1 ) for data of low regularity weighted Sobolev spaces was proven in [ 25 ] by analogy with the DNLS equation.
We indicate now another possible method to obtain the local well-posedness of ( 3.1 ) in a different functional setting by reducing it to a system of Benjamin-Ono type equations.
A natural idea is to transform ( 3.1 ) by diagonalizing the dispersive part. We denote $\widehat{A}(\xi)=i\xi\begin{pmatrix}0&1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2}\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.$ the Fourier transform of the dispersion matrix with eigenvalues $\pm i\xi(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}.$ In what follows we will denote $J_{\epsilon}=(I-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})^{1/2}.$ Setting $U=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
u\end{pmatrix}$ and $W=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta\\
v\end{pmatrix}=P^{-1}U,\quad P^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&J_{\epsilon}\\
1&-J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},$ the linear part of ( 3.1 ) is diagonalized as $W_{t}+\partial_{x}DW=0,$ where $D=\begin{pmatrix}J_{\epsilon}&0\\
0&-J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.$ Since $U=PW$ , where $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&-J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.$ one can therefore reduce ( 3.1 ) to the equivalent form (3.7) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\zeta_{t}+J_{\epsilon}\zeta_{x}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{1}(\zeta,v)=0,\\
v_{t}-J_{\epsilon}v_{x}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{2}(\zeta,v)=0.\end{array}\right.$ where $N_{1}(\zeta,v)=\partial_{x}[(\zeta+v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)]+J_{\epsilon}[J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta_{x}-v_{x})]$ and $N_{2}(\zeta,v)=\partial_{x}[(\zeta+v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)]-J_{\epsilon}[J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta_{x}-v_{x})]$ Since $(1+\epsilon\xi^{2})^{1/2}-\epsilon^{1/2}|\xi|=\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon\xi^{2})^{1/2}+\epsilon^{1/2}|\xi|},$ ( 3.7 ) writes (3.8) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\zeta_{t}+\epsilon^{1/2}\mathcal{H}\zeta_{xx}+R_{\epsilon}\zeta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{1}(\zeta,v)=0,\\
v_{t}-\epsilon^{1/2}\mathcal{H}v_{xx}-R_{\epsilon}v+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{2}(\zeta,v)=0.\end{array}\right.$ where $R_{\epsilon}$ is the (order zero) skew-adjoint operator with symbol $\frac{i\xi}{(1+\epsilon\xi^{2})^{1/2}+\epsilon^{1/2}|\xi|}.$ Note that the nonlinear term are similar but in a sense nicer than the quadratic term $uu_{x}$ of the Benjamin-Ono equation and thus we should apply for instance the method Ponce [ 35 ] used to solve the Cauchy problem for the Benjamin-Ono equation, that is the dispersive estimates on the group $e^{it\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}},$ since this method does not used the specific structure of the nonlinear term in the Benjamin-Ono equation. This would imply local well-posedness for $(\zeta_{0},v_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})^{2},s\geq 3/2,$ which corresponds to $(\eta_{0},u_{0})=(\zeta_{0}+v_{0},J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta_{0}-v_{0}))\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}).$ Note the difference with the functional setting of Theorem 3.1 . Similarly, it is likely that the method in [ 22 ] which leads to a local well-posedness theory in $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}),s>9/8$ for the Benjamin-Ono equation can be applied to ( 3.1 ) leading to a $H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}),s>9/8$ theory. Also the new method in [ 33 ] could lead to the
resolution of the Cauchy problem in the energy space $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})$ in the $(\zeta,v)$ variables. Those methods would however not enlarge the $O(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})$ lifespan. 6 6 6 On the other hand, Tao’s method [ 39 ] which leads to a $H^{1}(\mathbb{R})$ well-posedness theory for the Benjamin-Ono equation uses a gauge transform which strongly relies on the specific structure of the Benjamin-Ono equation and its generalization to ( 3.1 ) is problematic. In the two-dimensional case, the system writes (3.9) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot u+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})-\epsilon\nabla\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}=0,\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}=0.\end{array}\right.$ This system has also the Hamiltonian structure $\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{pmatrix}+J\text{grad}\;H_{\epsilon}(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})=0$ where $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{x}&\partial_{y}\\
\partial_{x}&0&0\\
\partial_{y}&0&0\end{pmatrix}.$ and $H(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\epsilon|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\eta^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}\eta)dxdy.$ Under a curl free assumption on ${\boldsymbol{u}}$ (which is natural since the Boussinesq systems are derived for potential flows), one can obtain the local well-posedness of ( 3.9 ) by "elementary" methods. Actually, when $\text{curl}\;{\boldsymbol{u}}=0,$ ( 3.9 ) becomes (3.10) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})-\epsilon\nabla\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}+\nabla\eta+\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}=0.\end{aligned}\right.$ Before going further, we present the following commutator estimates (see Theorems 3 and 6 in [ 28 ] ). Lemma 3.2 . Let $t_{0}>\frac{n}{2}$ , $-t_{0}<r\leq t_{0}+1$ . Then for all $s\geq 0$ , $f\in H^{t_{0}+1}\cap H^{s+r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and $u\in H^{s+r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ , there holds: (3.11) $|[\Lambda^{s},f]u|_{H^{r}}\leq C(|\nabla f|_{H^{t_{0}}}|u|_{H^{s+r-1}}+\langle|\nabla f|_{H^{s+r-1}}|u|_{H^{t_{0}}}\rangle_{s>t_{0}+1-r}),$ where $a+\langle b\rangle_{s>s_{0}}$ equals $a$ if $s\leq s_{0}$ while equals $a+b$ if $s>s_{0}$ . Consequently, taking $t_{0}=s>1$ and $r=0$ in ( 3.11 ), we have the following corollary. Corollary 3.3 . For $s>1$ , $f\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ , $g\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ , then (3.12) $|[\Lambda^{s},f]g|_{2}\lesssim|\nabla f|_{H^{s}}|g|_{H^{s-1}}.$ Going back to ( 3.10 ),
similarly to the one-dimensional case, we denote by $U=(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})^{T},$ and then define (3.13) $\|U\|_{X_{\epsilon}^{s}}=(|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}^{2}+|\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}^{2}+\epsilon|\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},$ and we obtain the following theorem. Theorem 3.4 . Let $s>1$ and $(\eta_{0},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0})\in X^{s}_{\epsilon}.$ Then there exists $T_{\epsilon}=O(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})$ and a unique solution $(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})\in C([0,T_{\epsilon}];X^{s}_{\epsilon})$ of ( 3.10 ) with initial data $(\eta_{0},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}).$ Moreover, $\sup_{t\in[0,T_{\epsilon}]}\|(\eta(\cdot,t),{\boldsymbol{u}}(\cdot,t))\|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}<c\|(\eta_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}.$ Proof. As in the one-dimensional case we will only provide the suitable a priori estimate. Taking the $L^{2}$ inner product of the first equation in ( 3.10 ) by $\Lambda^{2s}\eta$ and of the second equation by $(1-\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{2s}{\boldsymbol{u}}$ , and then integrating by parts, it results (3.14) $\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}^{2}+|\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}^{2}+\epsilon|\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\\
&=-\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,(1-\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2},\end{split}$ Now, we deal with the r.h.s terms in ( 3.14 ). We first get that (3.15) $\displaystyle(\Lambda^{s}\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}=(\Lambda^{s}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\eta)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+(\Lambda^{s}(\eta\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}$ $\displaystyle=([\Lambda^{s},{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla\eta)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+(\Lambda^{s}(\eta\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2},$ which together with ( 3.12 ) implies that (3.16) $\displaystyle|(\Lambda^{s}\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim|[\Lambda^{s},{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla\eta)|_{2}|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}+|\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{\infty}|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}^{2}+|\Lambda^{s}(\eta\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}})|_{2}|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\nabla\eta|_{H^{s-1}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}+|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}^{2}.$ For the second term on the r.h.s of ( 3.14 ), we have (3.17) $\displaystyle(\Lambda^{s}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,(1-\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}=([\Lambda^{s},{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}$ $\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\Lambda^{s}\nabla(u_{i}\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\nabla\boldsymbol{u})_{2}$ $\displaystyle=([\Lambda^{s},{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}$ $\displaystyle+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{2}([\Lambda^{s},u_{i}]\nabla\partial_{i}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}$ $\displaystyle+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\Lambda^{s}(\nabla u_{i}\partial_{i}{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}$ which along with ( 3.12 ) implies that (3.18) $|(\Lambda^{s}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,(1-\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}|\lesssim|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}\bigl(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\epsilon|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr).$ Denoting again $U=(\eta,\boldsymbol{u})^{T},$ we deduce from ( 3.14 ), ( 3.16 ) and ( 3.18 ) that (3.19) $\frac{d}{dt}\|U(t)\|_{X_{\epsilon}^{s}}\leq C\sqrt{\epsilon}\|U(t)\|_{X_{\epsilon}^{s}}^{2},$ from which, we infer that the maximal existence time interval is $[0,T_{\epsilon}]$ with $T_{\epsilon}=O(1/{\sqrt{\epsilon}})$ . As in the one-dimensional case one justifies the a priori estimates by a suitable approximation of the system (for instance by adding $(-\delta\Delta\eta_{t},-\delta\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t})^{T},\;\delta>0$ ). Uniqueness is obtained again by a Gronwall type argument and the strong continuity in time and the continuity of the flow map result from the Bona-Smith trick.
∎ As in the one-dimensional case, one has a better insight on the system by diagonalizing the linear part. The dispersion matrix is in Fourier variables $\widehat{A}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=i\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi_{1}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})&\xi_{2}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})\\
\xi_{1}&0&0\\
\\
\xi_{2}&0&0\end{pmatrix}.$ The corresponding eigenvalues are zero and $\lambda_{\pm}=\pm i|\xi|(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}$ with corresponding eigenvectors $E_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\\
\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix},\quad E_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\\
\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix},$ $\text{and}\quad E_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\\
\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}.$ Now we set $J_{\epsilon}=(I-\epsilon\Delta)^{1/2}$ and $R_{1},R_{2}$ the Fourier multiplier operator with respective symbols $i\xi_{1}/|\xi|,i\xi_{2}/|\xi|.$ We also denote $P=\begin{pmatrix}0&i&-i\\
-R_{2}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&R_{1}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&R_{1}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\\
R_{1}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&R_{2}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&R_{2}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}$ and $P^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&2R_{2}J_{\epsilon}&-2R_{1}J_{\epsilon}\\
-i&-R_{1}J_{\epsilon}&-R_{2}J_{\epsilon}\\
i&-R_{1}J_{\epsilon}&-R_{2}J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.$ Setting $U=(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})^{T}$ and $V=(\zeta,{\bf v})^{T}=P^{-1}U,$ ( 3.9 ) writes as $U_{t}+AU+\epsilon N(U)=0,$ which is transformed after diagonalizing the linear part, $V_{t}+DV+\epsilon P^{-1}N(PV)=0,$ or, setting $P^{-1}N(PV)=\tilde{N}(V),$ (3.20) $V_{t}+DV+\epsilon\tilde{N}(V)=0,$ where $D=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&i(-\Delta)^{1/2}J_{\epsilon}&0\\
0&0&-i(-\Delta)^{1/2}J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}$ We turn now to the nonlinear part. $N$ is given as a function of $U$ by $N(U)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\\
\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})\\
\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y}(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{2}\end{pmatrix},$ where we used the condition $\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{u}}=0$ in the second equality. On the other hand, $P^{-1}N(U)$ is given by $-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
i\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})+R_{1}J_{\epsilon}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{1})+R_{2}J_{\epsilon}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{2})\\
-i\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})+R_{1}J_{\epsilon}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{1})+R_{2}J_{\epsilon}({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla u_{2})\end{pmatrix}.$ To obtain the expression of $\tilde{N}(V)$ , we should express $(\eta,u_{1},u_{2})$ as $\eta=i(v_{1}-v_{2}),\quad u_{1}=-R_{2}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\zeta+R_{1}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(v_{1}+v_{2}),$ $u_{2}=R_{1}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\zeta+R_{2}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(v_{1}+v_{2}),$ and the nonlinearity is of the same type as in the one-dimensional case. Remark 3.1 . As in the case of the "KdV-KdV" system ( $a=c=1/6,b=d=0$ ) studied in [ 30 ] , it follows from our analysis that $\zeta=0$ if $\zeta_{0}$ is
smooth enough, since $\partial_{t}\zeta=0$ . Moreover, $\zeta=0\quad\iff\quad R_{2}u_{1}=R_{1}u_{2}\quad\iff\text{curl}\;{\boldsymbol{u}}=0.$ We observe that this condition makes sense, since our system is
derived from the water waves equations in the irrotational case. Note that ${\boldsymbol{u}}$ is the horizontal velocity at a certain height and it differs from the horizontal velocity at the free surface by an $O(\epsilon^{2})$ term. Also, since the equation for ${\boldsymbol{u}}$ writes $\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}=\nabla F$ , the condition $\text{curl}\;{\boldsymbol{u}}=0$ is preserved by the evolution. Remark 3.2 . The linear part in ( 3.20 ) is "Schrödinger like" for large frequencies (the symbol behaves as $\pm i\epsilon^{1/2}|\xi|^{2}$ as $|\xi|\to+\infty),$ and "wave like" for small frequencies (the symbol behaves as $\pm i|\xi|$ when $|\xi|\to 0).$ The quadratic terms however involves order one operators and this one could a priori think of applying the results on the Cauchy problem for quasilinear Schrödinger type equations (see for instance [ 23 ] ). Those methods however necessitate a high regularity on the data and it is unlikely that they could improve our local result. 3.2. A second Schrödinger type system We consider here the situation where $a=b=d=0,c<0,$ say $c=-1$ which again may occur only in the case of strong surface tension. It turns out that this system leads to serious difficulties, which are not present in the other Boussinesq systems, and that we will describe below, even to obtain the local well-posedness by "elementary" or more sophisticated methods using dispersion. We refer to Section 4 and 5 for
the long time existence issues. We consider first the one-dimensional system (3.21) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+u_{x}+\epsilon(u\eta)_{x}=0,\\
u_{t}+\eta_{x}+\epsilon uu_{x}-\epsilon\eta_{xxx}=0.\end{array}\right.$ The hamiltonian structure is now $\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
u\end{pmatrix}+J\text{grad}\;H_{\epsilon}(\eta,u)=0$ where $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $H_{\epsilon}(\eta,u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\epsilon|\eta_{x}|^{2}+\eta^{2}+u^{2}+\epsilon u^{2}\eta)dx.$ Similarly to the case $b=d>0,a=0,c=-1$ considered in [ 7 ] and for a related system in [ 10 ] , one can use the formal conservation of $H_{\epsilon}$ to derive a
global a priori estimate when $H_{\epsilon}(\eta_{0},u_{0})$ is small enough and $\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(1+\epsilon\eta_{0}(x))>0$ . First we derive as in [ 7 , 9 ] a $L^{\infty}$ bound on $\eta$ for solutions $(\eta,u)\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))$ satisfying the above non-cavitation condition. Actually, one writes (3.22) $\begin{split}\eta^{2}(x,t)&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\eta||\eta_{x}|dx=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\epsilon}|\eta||\eta_{x}|dx\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{R}(\eta^{2}+\epsilon|\eta_{x}|^{2})dx\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}|H_{\epsilon}(\eta,u)|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}|H_{\epsilon}(\eta_{0},u_{0})|\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}$ Using ( 3.22 ) and the conservation of $H_{\epsilon}$ imply a (formal ) $H^{1}\times L^{2}$ bound on $(\eta,u)$ provided $H_{\epsilon}(\eta_{0},u_{0})$ is small enough, that is (3.23) $H_{\epsilon}(\eta_{0},u_{0})<\epsilon^{-3/2}.$ Unfortunately, and contrary to the case $b=d>0,a=0,c<0$ (see [ 7 ] ), one cannot use the above bounds to get a global well-posedness result, say by a compactness method applied to a regularization of the system. The obstruction is that one cannot pass to the limit of the term $\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}(u^{2})$ in the second equation only from a $L^{2}$ bound on $u.$ To obtain an equivalent "diagonal" system, we proceed as in the other "Schrödinger type system", setting now $\widehat{A}(\xi)=i\xi\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.$ with the same eigenvalues $\pm i\xi(1+\epsilon|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}.$ In the Notation of the previous section, one has still $D=\begin{pmatrix}J_{\epsilon}&0\\
0&-J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.$ and now $P=\begin{pmatrix}J_{\epsilon}^{-1}&-J_{\epsilon}^{-1}\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},$ $P^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}J_{\epsilon}&1\\
-J_{\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},$ Setting again $U=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
u\end{pmatrix}$ and $W=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta\\
v\end{pmatrix}=P^{-1}U,$ one can therefore reduce ( 3.21 ) to the equivalent form (3.24) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\zeta_{t}+J_{\epsilon}\zeta_{x}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{1}(\zeta,v)=0,\\
v_{t}-J_{\epsilon}v_{x}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{2}(\zeta,v)=0.\end{array}\right.$ where $N_{1}(\zeta,v)=\partial_{x}J_{\epsilon}[(\zeta+v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)]+(\zeta+v)(\zeta+v)_{x}$ and $N_{2}(\zeta,v)=-\partial_{x}J_{\epsilon}[(\zeta+v)J_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\zeta-v)]+(\zeta+v)(\zeta+v)_{x}.$ We can also write ( 3.24 ) as (3.25) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\zeta_{t}+\epsilon^{1/2}\mathcal{H}\zeta_{xx}+R_{\epsilon}\zeta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{1}(\zeta,v)=0,\\
v_{t}-\epsilon^{1/2}\mathcal{H}v_{xx}-R_{\epsilon}v+\frac{\epsilon}{2}N_{2}(\zeta,v)=0.\end{array}\right.$ where again $R_{\epsilon}$ is the order zero skew-adjoint operator with symbol $\frac{i\xi}{(1+\epsilon\xi^{2})^{1/2}+\epsilon^{1/2}|\xi|}.$ Note that the nonlinearity is worse than in the case $a=-1$ and even the local theory does not seem to be straightforward using this formulation. We turn now to the two-dimensional case that is (3.26) $\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})=0,\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}-\epsilon\nabla\Delta\eta=0.\end{array}\right.$ The Hamiltonian structure is now $\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{pmatrix}+J\text{grad}\;H_{\epsilon}(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})=0$ where $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{x}&\partial_{y}\\
\partial_{x}&0&0\\
\partial_{y}&0&0\end{pmatrix}.$ and $H(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\epsilon|\nabla\eta|^{2}+\eta^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}\eta)dxdy.$ As in the one-dimensional case,
one can express ( 3.21 ) on the equivalent form (3.27) $V_{t}+DV+\tilde{N}(V)=0,$ where again $U=(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})^{T}$ , $V=(\zeta,{\bf v})^{T}$ , $D=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&i(-\Delta)^{1/2}J_{\epsilon}&0\\
0&0&-i(-\Delta)^{1/2}J_{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}$ and $\tilde{N}(V)$ is expressed as $\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-iJ_{\epsilon}[\partial_{x}(u_{1}\eta)+\partial_{y}(u_{2}\eta)]-\frac{1}{2}[R_{1}\partial_{x}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+R_{2}\partial_{y}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}]\\
iJ_{\epsilon}[\partial_{x}(u_{1}\eta)+\partial_{y}(u_{2}\eta)]-\frac{1}{2}[R_{1}\partial_{x}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}+R_{2}\partial_{y}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}]\ \end{pmatrix}$ with $\eta=iJ_{\epsilon}^{-1}(v_{1}-v_{2}),\quad u_{1}=-R_{2}\zeta+R_{1}(v_{1}+v_{2}),\quad u_{2}=R_{1}\zeta+R_{2}(v_{1}+v_{2}).$ 3.3. Comparison between the two Schrödinger type systems The previous considerations display the difficulties of the Cauchy problem in the case $a=b=d=0,c<0.$ We indicate here how to reduce it to the case $b=d=c=0,a<0$ modulo $O(\epsilon^{2})$ terms. Let us consider for instance the one-dimensional case (3.28) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t}+u_{x}+\epsilon(\eta u)_{x}=0,\\
&u_{t}+\eta_{x}+\epsilon uu_{x}-\epsilon\eta_{xxx}=0.\end{aligned}\right.$ Setting $\tilde{\eta}=(1-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})\eta=J_{\epsilon}^{2}\eta,$ ( 3.28 ) can be rewritten as follows : (3.29) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})u_{x}+\epsilon(\tilde{\eta}u)_{x}=\epsilon^{2}(2\eta_{xx}u_{x}+3\eta_{x}u_{xx}+\eta u_{xxx}),\\
&u_{t}+\tilde{\eta}_{x}+\epsilon uu_{x}=0,\end{aligned}\right.$ that is (3.30) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})u_{x}+\epsilon(\tilde{\eta}u)_{x}\\
&\quad=\epsilon^{2}(2(J_{\epsilon}^{-2}\tilde{\eta}_{xx})u_{x}+3(J_{\epsilon}^{-2}\tilde{\eta}_{x})u_{xx}+(J_{\epsilon}^{-2}\tilde{\eta})u_{xxx}),\\
&u_{t}+\tilde{\eta}+\epsilon uu_{x}=0,\end{aligned}\right.$ Discarding the $O(\epsilon^{2})$ terms, ( 3.29 ) reduces to (3.31) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\partial_{x}^{2})u_{x}+\epsilon(\tilde{\eta}u)_{x}=0,\\
&u_{t}+\epsilon\tilde{\eta}_{x}+\epsilon uu_{x}=0.\end{aligned}\right.$ which is exactly the case $b=c=d=0,a=-1.$ Similarly, we can consider the two-dimensional case (3.32) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t}+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})=0,\\
&{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\eta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})-\epsilon\nabla\Delta\eta={\bf 0}.\end{aligned}\right.$ Setting $\tilde{\eta}=(1-\epsilon\Delta)\eta=J_{\epsilon}^{2}\eta,$ ( 3.32 ) can be rewritten as follows : (3.33) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\Delta)\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\tilde{\eta}{\boldsymbol{u}})=\epsilon^{2}\bigl(\Delta\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})-\nabla\cdot(\Delta\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})\bigr),\\
&{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\tilde{\eta}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})=0,\end{aligned}\right.$ that is (3.34) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\Delta)\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\tilde{\eta}{\boldsymbol{u}})=\epsilon^{2}\bigl(\Delta\nabla\cdot({\boldsymbol{u}}J_{\epsilon}^{-2}\tilde{\eta})-\nabla\cdot({\boldsymbol{u}}\Delta J_{\epsilon}^{-2}\tilde{\eta}\bigr),\\
&{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\tilde{\eta}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})=0,\end{aligned}\right.$ Discarding the $O(\epsilon^{2})$ terms, ( 3.33 ) reduces to (3.35) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\eta}_{t}+(1-\epsilon\Delta)\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\tilde{\eta}{\boldsymbol{u}})=0,\\
&{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t}+\nabla\tilde{\eta}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla(|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})={\bf 0}\end{aligned}\right.$ which is exactly the case $b=c=d=0,a=-1.$ The bad structure of the nonlinear terms in ( 3.30 ), ( 3.34 ) (or ( 3.25 ), ( 3.27 )) explain why solving the Cauchy problem for systems ( 3.21 ) or ( 3.26 ) is so difficult, despite their apparent simplicity. One could notice that we always lose one derivative for $\eta$ or $\boldsymbol{u}$ . Thus, to solve the case $a=b=d=0,c<0$ , we turn to quasilinearize the system by applying $\partial_{t}^{k}$ instead of the usual $\partial_{x}^{\alpha}$ . We shall discuss details in the following section. 4. Long time existence for some Boussinesq systems We first give a complete proof for some systems considered in [ 36 ] (in particular the two-dimensional version of the system considered in [ 4 , 38 ] ) and apply the same symmetrization techniques to study the long time existence of solutions (in a smaller Sobolev space) of one of the "Schrödinger type systems" described in the previous section. We then consider the more delicate case $a=b=d=0,c<0.$ We associate to ( 1.2 ) the initial data (4.1) $\eta|_{t=0}=\eta_{0},\quad{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{t=0}={\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}.$ 4.1. The case $a=c=d=0,b>0$ with condition $\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\;{\boldsymbol{u}}=0$ Before going further, we state some technical lemmas and definitions. Definition 4.1 . For any $s\in\mathbb{R}$ , $k\in\mathbb{N}$ , $\epsilon\in(0,1)$ , the Banach space $X^{s}_{\epsilon^{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ is defined as $H^{s+k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ equipped with the norm: $\displaystyle|u|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{k}}}^{2}=|u|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\epsilon^{k}|u|_{H^{s+k}}^{2}$ Lemma 4.2 . For any $i,k\in\mathbb{N}$ and $0<i<k$ ,
there holds the following interpolation inequality: (4.2) $\epsilon^{\frac{i}{2}}|f|_{H^{s+i}}\lesssim|f|_{H^{s}}^{1-\frac{i}{k}}\bigl(\epsilon^{\frac{k}{2}}|f|_{H^{s+k}}\bigr)^{\frac{i}{k}}\lesssim|f|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{k}}}.$ Theorem 4.3 . Let $b>0,a=c=d=0$ . $n=1,2$ , $s>1+\frac{n}{2}$ .
Assume that $\eta_{0}\in X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}\in X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ with $\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}=0$ when $n=2$ , satisfy the (non-cavitation) condition (4.3) $1+\epsilon\eta_{0}\geq H>0,\quad H\in(0,1),$ Then there exists a constant $\tilde{c}_{0}$ such that for any $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}=\frac{1-H}{\tilde{c}_{0}(|\eta_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\bf u}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}})}$ , there
exists $T>0$ independent of $\epsilon$ , such that ( 1.2 )-( 4.1 ) has a unique solution $(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})^{T}$ with $\eta\in C([0,T/\epsilon];X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ and ${\boldsymbol{u}}\in C([0,T/\epsilon];X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ . Moreover, (4.4) $\max_{t\in[0,T/\epsilon]}(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}})\leq\tilde{c}(|\eta_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}).$ Here $\tilde{c}=C(H^{-1})$ and $\tilde{c}_{0}=C(H^{-1})$ are nondecreasing functions of their argument.
And in what follows, without confusion, we denote $\tilde{c}=C(H^{-1})$ a
nondecreasing constant depending on $H^{-1}$ .
Otherwise, we denote $\tilde{c}_{i}$ (i=0,1,2,…) constants having the same
properties as $\tilde{c}$ . Proof. The proof follows the same method used in [ 36 ] , that is to obtain energy estimates on a suitable symmetrized linearized system followed by an iterative scheme. Here we only give the a priori estimates on the full nonlinear system and in the two-dimensional case.
Since $c=d=0$ and $\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}=0$ , we deduce from the second equation of ( 1.2 ) that (4.5) $\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{u}}=0,\quad\text{for}\quad t>0.$ Then using ( 4.5 ), ( 1.2 ) becomes (4.6) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta+\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta{\boldsymbol{u}})-b\epsilon\Delta\partial_{t}\eta=0,\\
&\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}+\nabla\eta+\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}={\bf 0}.\end{aligned}\right.$ Denoting by $U=(\eta,{\boldsymbol{u}})$ , ( 4.6 ) is rewritten in the condensed form as (4.7) $(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{t}U+M(U,D)U={\bf 0},$ where $\displaystyle M(U,D)=\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla&(1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1}&(1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2}\\
(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{1}&(1-b\epsilon\Delta)(\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla)&0\\
(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{2}&0&(1-b\epsilon\Delta)(\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla)\end{pmatrix}.$ The symmetrizer of $M(U,D)$ is $\displaystyle S_{U}(D)=\begin{pmatrix}1-b\epsilon\Delta&0&0\\
0&1+\epsilon\eta&0\\
0&0&1+\epsilon\eta\end{pmatrix}.$ We define the energy functional associated to ( 4.7 ) as (4.8) $\displaystyle E_{s}(U)$ $\displaystyle=((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle=((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}.$ Assume that (4.9) $1+\epsilon\eta\geq H>0,\quad\epsilon|\eta|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leq\kappa_{H}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[0,\bar{T}]$ with $\kappa_{H}$ sufficiently small,
and (4.10) $\max_{0\leq t\leq\bar{T}}E_{s}(U)\leq C_{0},$ for some constant $C_{0}$ .
The assumptions ( 4.9 ) and ( 4.10 ) hold provided that ( 4.3 ) holds and $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}\ll 1$ (one can refer to [ 36 ] ). Under the conditions ( 4.9 ), it is easy to check that (4.11) $E_{s}(U)\sim|\eta|_{X_{\epsilon^{2}}^{s}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X_{\epsilon}^{s}}^{2}.$ The proof of ( 4.11 ) is similar to that in [ 36 ] and we omit it. A standard energy estimate leads to (4.12) $\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)=2((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle+((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\partial_{t}S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}-b\epsilon\bigl([S_{U}(D),\Delta]\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\Lambda^{s}U_{t}\bigr)_{2}$ $\displaystyle=-2(\Lambda^{s}\bigl(M(U,D)U\bigr)\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}+((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\epsilon\partial_{t}\eta\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}$ $\displaystyle-b\epsilon^{2}\bigl([\eta,\Delta]\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)_{2}$ $\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{=}I+II+III.$ Estimate for $I$ . Firstly, one gets $\displaystyle I=-2([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}-2(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}\underset{\text{def}}{=}I_{1}+I_{2}.$ For $I_{1}$ , one has $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I_{1}&=-2([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla\eta+[\Lambda^{s},\epsilon\eta]\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}\\
&\quad-2((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\bigl([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}\\
&\underset{\text{def}}{=}I_{11}+I_{12}.\end{aligned}$ Thanks to Lemma 3.2 , it is easy to get that for $s>2$ , (4.13) $\displaystyle|I_{11}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\bigl(|[\Lambda^{s},\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla\eta|_{2}+|[\Lambda^{s},\epsilon\eta]\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}\bigr)|(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}(|\eta|_{H^{s}}+\epsilon|\eta|_{H^{s+2}})\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}.$ For $I_{12}$ , integrating by parts, there holds $\displaystyle I_{12}=-2([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}-2b\epsilon(\nabla\bigl([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}]\cdot\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)\,|\,\nabla\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr))_{2}$ which along with ( 4.9 ) and Lemma 3.2 implies that (4.14) $\displaystyle|I_{12}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\epsilon|\eta|_{\infty})\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}^{3}+\epsilon^{2}|{\bf u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigl((1+\epsilon|\eta|_{\infty})|\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}+\epsilon|\nabla\eta|_{\infty}|\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{3}.$ Then we get by ( 4.13 ) and ( 4.14 ) that (4.15) $|I_{1}|\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}).$ For $I_{2}$ , due to the expressions of $M(U,D)$ and $S_{U}(D)$ , we get that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I_{2}=&-2(\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-2((1-b\epsilon\Delta)(\epsilon{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}\\
&-2\{((1+\epsilon\eta)\nabla\cdot\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+((1-b\epsilon\Delta)\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}\}\\
\underset{\text{def}}{=}&I_{21}+I_{22}+I_{23}.\end{aligned}$ Integrating by parts, one gets that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &I_{21}=\epsilon(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+b\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-2b\epsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{i}\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}\\
&I_{22}=\epsilon(\nabla\cdot\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta){\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}{\bf u})_{2}+2b\epsilon^{3}(({\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla)\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}(\partial_{i}\eta\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}))_{2}\\
&\qquad-2b\epsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{i}\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}+b\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\cdot\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta){\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2},\\
&I_{23}=2\epsilon(\nabla\eta\cdot\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,(1-b\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}.\end{aligned}$ Then thanks to ( 4.9 ), ( 4.10 ), ( 4.11 ) and ( 4.2 ), there holds (4.16) $|I_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigr).$ Thanks to ( 4.15 ) and ( 4.16 ), we obtain (4.17) $|I|\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigr).$ Estimate for $II$ . Integrating by parts, we have $\displaystyle II=\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\partial_{t}\eta\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}})_{2}+b\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}\,|\,\nabla(\partial_{t}\eta\Lambda^{s}{\boldsymbol{u}}))_{2}$ which along with ( 4.2 ) implies that (4.18) $|II|\lesssim\epsilon|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}.$ Estimate for $III$ . Thanks to Lemma 3.2 , we get that (4.19) $|III|\lesssim\epsilon^{2}|\nabla\eta|_{H^{s-1}}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s+1}}|\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{2}\lesssim\epsilon|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}|\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}.$ Combining ( 4.12 ), ( 4.17 ), ( 4.18 ) and ( 4.19 ), we obtain that (4.20) $\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}\bigr)\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigr).$ Thanks to the equations of ( 4.6 ), one gets by using ( 4.9 ) and ( 4.10 ) that (4.21) $\displaystyle|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\epsilon|\eta|_{\infty})|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{\infty}|\eta|_{H^{s}}+|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}},$ which along with ( 4.20 ) implies that (4.22) $\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+|{\bf u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}\bigr)\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigr).$ Then due to ( 4.11 ), there holds $\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(E_{s}(U)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{1}\epsilon E_{s}(U),$ which gives rise to (4.23) $\bigl(E_{s}(U)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{1-C_{1}\epsilon t\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq 2\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}},$ for any $t\leq\frac{1}{2C_{1}\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{T}{\epsilon}$ with $T=\frac{1}{2C_{1}\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}$ .
This completes the proof of Theorem 4.3 .
∎ 4.2. Case $d>0,a=b=c=0$ . Theorem 4.4 . Let $d>0,a=b=c=0$ . $n=1,2$ , $s>1+\frac{n}{2}$ .
Assume that $\eta_{0}\in X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ satisfy the (non-cavitation) condition (4.24) $1+\epsilon\eta_{0}\geq H>0,\quad H\in(0,1),$ Then there exists a constant $\tilde{c}_{0}$ such that for any $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}=\frac{1-H}{\tilde{c}_{0}(|\eta_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}})}$ , there
exists $T>0$ independent of $\epsilon$ , such that ( 1.2 )-( 4.1 ) has a unique solution $(\eta,\boldsymbol{u})^{T}$ with $\eta\in C([0,T/\epsilon];X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ and $\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T/\epsilon];X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ . Moreover, (4.25) $\max_{t\in[0,T/\epsilon]}(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}})\leq\tilde{c}(|\eta_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}).$ Remark 4.1 . As was previously mentioned, one gets global well-posedness in the one-dimensional case ( [ 4 , 38 ] ) in a different functional setting though but the method of proof in [ 4 , 38 ] does not seem to adapt to the two-dimensional case since it relies strongly on properties of the one-dimensional hyperbolic Saint-Venant (shallow water) system. Proof. The proof also follows the same method used in [ 36 ] . Here we only give the a priori estimates . For $d>0,a=b=c=0$ , we rewrite ( 1.2 ) in two-dimensional space as follows: (4.26) $\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta+\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}+\epsilon\nabla\cdot(\eta\boldsymbol{u})=0,\\
&\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\eta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})-d\epsilon\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},\end{aligned}\right.$ Denoting by $U=(\eta,\boldsymbol{u})$ , ( 4.26 ) is equivalent to the following condensed system (4.27) $(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{t}U+M(U,D)U=0,$ where $\displaystyle M(U,D)=\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)&(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1}\bigr)&(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2}\bigr)\\
\partial_{1}&\epsilon u_{1}\partial_{1}&\epsilon u_{2}\partial_{1}\\
\partial_{2}&\epsilon u_{1}\partial_{2}&\epsilon u_{2}\partial_{2}\end{pmatrix}.$ The symmetrizer $S_{U}(D)$ for $M(U,D)$ is defined by $\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&\epsilon u_{1}&\epsilon u_{2}\\
\epsilon u_{1}&(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)&0\\
\epsilon u_{2}&0&(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&d\epsilon^{3}u_{1}u_{1}\Delta&d\epsilon^{3}u_{1}u_{2}\Delta\\
0&d\epsilon^{3}u_{1}u_{2}\Delta&d\epsilon^{3}u_{2}u_{2}\Delta\end{pmatrix}.$ We define the energy functional associated to ( 4.27 ) as (4.28) $E_{s}(U)=((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ Assume that (4.29) $1+\epsilon\eta\geq H>0,\quad\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leq\kappa_{H}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[0,\bar{T}]$ with $\kappa_{H}$ sufficiently small, and (4.30) $\max_{0\leq t\leq\bar{T}}E_{s}(U)\leq C_{0},$ for some constants $C_{0}$ .
The assumptions ( 4.29 ) and ( 4.30 ) also hold provided that ( 4.24 ) holds and $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}\ll 1$ (one can refer to [ 36 ] ).
Under the assumption ( 4.29 ), it is not difficult to check that (4.31) $E_{s}(U)\sim|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}.$ As usual, a standard computation shows that (4.32) $\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)=-(\Lambda^{s}\bigl(M(U,D)U\bigr)\,|\,\bigl(S_{U}(D)+S_{U}(D)^{*}\bigr)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle-d\epsilon([S_{U}(D)^{*},\Delta]\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}U)_{2}+((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\partial_{t}S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{=}I+II+III,$ where $S_{U}(D)^{*}$ is the adjoint matrix of $S_{U}(D)$ . Estimate for $I$ . One has that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I=&-([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,\bigl(S_{U}(D)+S_{U}(D)^{*}\bigr)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}\\
&-(\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\bigl(S_{U}(D)+S_{U}(D)^{*}\bigr)\bigl(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U\bigr))_{2}\\
\underset{\text{def}}{=}&I_{1}+I_{2}\end{aligned}$ Estimate for $I_{1}$ . Using the expressions of $M(U,D)$ and $S_{U}(D)$ , one gets that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}=([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)]\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}\\
&\quad+([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\eta\nabla)]\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}\\
&\quad+\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}([\Lambda^{s},\epsilon u_{j}]\partial_{i}u_{j}\,|\,\epsilon u_{i}\Lambda^{s}\eta+(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}u_{i}+d\epsilon^{3}u_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}\\
&\underset{\text{def}}{=}I_{11}+I_{12}+I_{13}.\end{aligned}$ Integrating by parts, there hold $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &I_{11}=\epsilon([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+d\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\bigl([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla\eta\bigr)\,|\,\nabla\bigl(\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr))_{2}\\
&I_{12}=\epsilon([\Lambda^{s},\eta]\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+d\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\bigl([\Lambda^{s},\eta]\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\,|\,\nabla\bigl(\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr))_{2},\end{aligned}$ which along with ( 4.29 ), ( 4.30 ), ( 3.11 ) and ( 4.2 ) imply that (4.33) $\displaystyle|I_{11}|+|I_{12}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}\bigl(|\eta|_{H^{s}}+\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\bigl(|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s-1}}|\nabla\eta|_{H^{s}}+|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\nabla\eta|_{H^{s-1}}\bigr)\bigl(|\eta|_{H^{s+1}}+\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s+1}}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ Thanks to ( 3.11 ), ( 4.2 ), ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.31 ), there holds $\displaystyle|I_{13}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigl(\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+d\epsilon^{3}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{3}\bigr)\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr),$ which along with ( 4.33 ) shows that $\displaystyle|([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ The same estimate holds for term $([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,S_{U}(D)^{*}\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ . Then we obtain that (4.34) $|I_{1}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ Estimate for $I_{2}$ . For $I_{2}$ , we first calculate $S_{U}(D)(M(U,D))=A(U,D)=(a_{ij})$ as follows $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &a_{11}=\epsilon(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla=2\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla-d\epsilon^{2}\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla),\\
&a_{12}=(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1})+\epsilon^{2}u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\\
&a_{13}=(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2})+\epsilon^{2}u_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\\
&a_{21}=(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{1}+\epsilon^{2}u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot-d\epsilon^{3}u_{1}[\Delta,\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla,\\
&a_{22}=\epsilon u_{1}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1})+\epsilon(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\partial_{1})+d\epsilon^{4}u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{1}\nabla),\\
&a_{23}=\epsilon u_{1}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2})+\epsilon(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{2}\partial_{1})+d\epsilon^{4}u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{2}\nabla),\\
&a_{31}=(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{2}+\epsilon^{2}u_{2}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)+d\epsilon^{3}u_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta,\\
&a_{32}=\epsilon u_{2}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1})+\epsilon(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\partial_{2})+d\epsilon^{4}u_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{1}\nabla),\\
&a_{33}=\epsilon u_{2}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2})+\epsilon(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{2}\partial_{2})+d\epsilon^{4}u_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{2}\nabla).\end{aligned}$ Now , we calculate $(S_{U}(D)(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}=(A(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ . For $a_{11}$ , one has $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(a_{11}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}=2\epsilon(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-d\epsilon^{2}(\Delta(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}\\
&=-\epsilon(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-\frac{1}{2}d\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+d\epsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{i}\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2},\end{aligned}$ which shows that (4.35) $|(a_{11}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}.$ For $a_{22}$ , one gets $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(a_{22}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}=\epsilon\{(u_{1}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\\
&\quad+((1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\}+d\epsilon^{4}(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{1}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\\
&=-\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\epsilon\partial_{1}\eta(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})+(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\partial_{1}u_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}))_{2}\\
&\quad-d\epsilon^{4}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}(u_{1}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\partial_{i}(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}))_{2}\end{aligned}$ which along with ( 4.29 ),( 4.30 ) and ( 4.2 ) gives rise to (4.36) $|(a_{22}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}.$ The same estimate holds for term $(a_{33}\Lambda^{s}u_{2}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}$ . For $a_{12}$ and $a_{21}$ , one calculates that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(a_{12}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+(a_{21}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\\
&=\{((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+((1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\}\\
&\quad+\{\epsilon^{2}(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+\epsilon^{2}(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}-d\epsilon^{3}(u_{1}[\Delta,\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\}\\
&=-\epsilon((1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\partial_{1}\eta\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}-\epsilon^{2}(\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u})\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}\\
&\quad+\epsilon^{3}(\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,[\Delta,\boldsymbol{u}](u_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}))_{2},\end{aligned}$ which along with ( 4.29 ), ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.2 ) implies (4.37) $\displaystyle|(a_{12}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+(a_{21}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}+\epsilon^{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{3}|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ The same estimate holds for $(a_{13}\Lambda^{s}u_{2}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}+(a_{31}\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}$ . At last, for $a_{23}$ and $a_{32}$ , one estimates that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(a_{23}\Lambda^{s}u_{2}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}+(a_{32}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\\
&=\epsilon\{(u_{1}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{2}\Lambda^{s}u_{2}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}+((1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\partial_{2}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\}\\
&\quad+\epsilon\{((1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{2}\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{2})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}+(u_{2}(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl((1+\epsilon\eta)\partial_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\}\\
&\quad+d\epsilon^{4}\{(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{2}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{2})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}+(u_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Delta(u_{1}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\}\\
&=-\epsilon(\epsilon\partial_{2}\eta(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})+(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\partial_{2}u_{1}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\\
&\quad-\epsilon(\epsilon\partial_{1}\eta(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(u_{2}\Lambda^{s}u_{2})+(1+\epsilon\eta)(1-d\epsilon\Delta)(\partial_{1}u_{2}\Lambda^{s}u_{2})\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}\\
&\quad-d\epsilon^{4}\{(\nabla u_{2}\cdot\Delta(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u}\Lambda^{s}u_{1})+u_{2}\Delta\bigl(\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\boldsymbol{u})\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\\
&\quad-(2u_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{i}(u_{1}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1})+u_{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot u_{1}\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}\},\end{aligned}$ which together with ( 4.29 ), ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.2 ) leads to (4.38) $\displaystyle|(a_{23}\Lambda^{s}u_{2}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{1})_{2}+(a_{32}\Lambda^{s}u_{1}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}u_{2})_{2}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon(1+\epsilon|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}})|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{3}+\epsilon^{3}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{5}\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{3}.$ Thanks to ( 4.35 ), ( 4.36 ), ( 4.37 ) and ( 4.38 ), we obtain that $\displaystyle|(S_{U}(D)(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)^{3},$ provided that there hold ( 4.29 ) and ( 4.30 ). The same estimate holds for $(S_{U}(D)^{*}(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ . Then we obtain
that (4.39) $|I_{2}|\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)^{3}.$ Due to ( 4.34 ) and ( 4.39 ), we get that (4.40) $|I|\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)^{3}.$ Estimate for $II$ . Using the expression of $S_{U}(D)$ , one obtains that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &II=-d\epsilon\Bigl(\epsilon([\boldsymbol{u},\Delta]\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}\eta)_{2}+\epsilon([\boldsymbol{u},\Delta]\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}\\
&\quad+\epsilon((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\bigl([\eta,\Delta]\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+d\epsilon^{3}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}(\Delta\bigl([u_{i}u_{j},\Delta]\Lambda^{s}u_{j}\bigr)\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}u_{i})_{2}\Bigr).\end{aligned}$ Along the same line as previous work, by virtue of ( 3.11 ), ( 4.2 ), ( 4.29 ), ( 4.30 ) and integrating by parts, we finally get that (4.41) $|II|\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr)\bigl(|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}+|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr).$ Estimate for $III$ . Using the expression of $S_{U}(D)$ again, one gets that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &III=\epsilon((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+\epsilon((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\Lambda^{s}\eta)_{2}\\
&\qquad+\epsilon((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{t}\eta(1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+d\epsilon^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{2}((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}u_{j}\,|\,\partial_{t}(u_{j}\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\Delta\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}.\end{aligned}$ Note that $\displaystyle((1-d\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}=(\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+d\epsilon(\nabla\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\nabla(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}))_{2}$ Then ( 4.29 ), ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.2 ) leads to (4.42) $|III|\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr)\bigl(|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}+|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)$ Combining ( 4.32 ), ( 4.40 ), ( 4.41 ) and ( 4.42 ), we obtain that (4.43) $\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}+|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ Thanks to ( 4.26 ), we get by using ( 4.29 ) and ( 4.30 ) that (4.44) $|\partial_{t}\eta|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon}}+|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s-1}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\lesssim|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}},$ which along with ( 4.43 ) implies (4.45) $\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}\bigr)\bigl(|\eta|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon^{2}}}^{2}\bigr).$ Then due to ( 4.31 ), there holds $\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(E_{s}(U)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{1}\epsilon E_{s}(U).$ Similarly as the proof to Theorem 4.3 , there exists $T=\frac{1}{2C_{1}\bigl(E_{s}(U_{0})\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}$ such that ( 4.25 ) holds.
This completes the proof of Theorem 4.4 .
∎ We now turn to the "Schrödinger like" Boussinesq systems. 4.3. The case $b=d=c=0,a<0$ This case can be treated by following the lines developed in [ 36 ] . For the sake of completeness we provide some details now. Theorem 4.5 . Let $b=c=d=0,a=-1$ , $n=1,2$ , $s>2+\frac{n}{2}$ .
Assume that $\eta_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ satisfy the (non-cavitation) condition (4.46) $1+\epsilon\eta_{0}\geq H>0,\quad H\in(0,1),$ Then there exists a constant $\tilde{c}_{0}$ such that for any $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}=\frac{1-H}{\tilde{c}_{0}(|\eta_{0}|_{H^{s}}+|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}})}$ , there
exists $T>0$ independent of $\epsilon$ , such that ( 1.2 )-( 4.1 ) has a unique solution $(\eta,\boldsymbol{u})^{T}$ with $\eta\in C([0,T/\epsilon];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ and $\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T/\epsilon];X^{s}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ . Moreover, (4.47) $\max_{t\in[0,T/\epsilon]}(|\eta|_{H^{s}}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}})\leq\tilde{c}(|\eta_{0}|_{H^{s}}+|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}).$ Proof. We only sketch the proof of the two-dimensional case. For $b=c=d=0,a=-1$ , we firstly rewrite the two-dimensional version of ( 1.2 ) in the following condensed system (4.48) $\partial_{t}U+M(U,D)U=0,$ where $U=(\eta,\boldsymbol{u})^{T}$ , and $\displaystyle M(U,D)=\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla&(1+\epsilon\eta-\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{1}&(1+\epsilon\eta-\epsilon\Delta)\partial_{2}\\
\partial_{1}&\epsilon u_{1}\partial_{1}&\epsilon u_{2}\partial_{1}\\
\partial_{2}&\epsilon u_{1}\partial_{2}&\epsilon u_{2}\partial_{2}\end{pmatrix}.$ The symmetrizer $S_{U}(D)$ for $M(U,D)$ is defined by $\displaystyle S_{U}(D)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\epsilon u_{1}&\epsilon u_{2}\\
\epsilon u_{1}&1+\epsilon\eta-\epsilon\Delta&0\\
\epsilon u_{2}&0&1+\epsilon\eta-\epsilon\Delta.\end{pmatrix}$ We define the energy functional associated to ( 4.48 ) as (4.49) $E_{s}(U)=(\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ Assume that (4.50) $1+\epsilon\eta\geq H>0,\quad\epsilon|\eta|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leq\kappa_{H}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[0,\bar{T}]$ with $\kappa_{H}$ sufficiently small, and (4.51) $\max_{0\leq t\leq\bar{T}}E_{s}(U)\leq C_{0},$ for some constants $C_{0}$ .
The assumptions ( 4.50 ) and ( 4.51 ) also hold provided that ( 4.46 ) holds and $\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}\ll 1$ (one can refer to [ 36 ] ).
Under the assumption ( 4.50 ), it is not difficult to check that (4.52) $E_{s}(U)\sim|\eta|_{H^{s}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}.$ As usual, we get by a standard energy estimate that (4.53) $\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}(U)=2(\Lambda^{s}\partial_{t}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}+(\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\partial_{t}S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle=-2(\Lambda^{s}\bigl(M(U,D)U\bigr)\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}+(\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,\partial_{t}S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}$ $\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{=}I+II.$ Estimate for $II$ . Using the expression of $S_{U}(D)$ yields that $\displaystyle II=2\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}\eta\,|\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}+\epsilon(\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{t}\eta\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2},$ which implies that for $s>3$ , (4.54) $|II|\lesssim\epsilon|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s-1}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}+\epsilon|\partial_{t}\eta|_{H^{s-2}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2}.$ Estimate for $I$ . We first have that $\displaystyle I=-2([\Lambda^{s},M(U,D)]U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}-2(M(U,D)\Lambda^{s}U\,|\,S_{U}(D)\Lambda^{s}U)_{2}\underset{\text{def}}{=}I_{1}+I_{2}.$ Estimate for $I_{1}$ . For $I_{1}$ , we get that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I_{1}&=-2\epsilon([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla\eta+[\Lambda^{s},\eta]\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\Lambda^{s}\eta+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u})_{2}\\
&\quad-2\epsilon\sum_{j=1}^{2}([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,u_{j}\Lambda^{s}\eta+(1+\epsilon\eta-\epsilon\Delta)\Lambda^{s}u_{j})_{2}\\
&\underset{\text{def}}{=}I_{11}+I_{12}.\end{aligned}$ Thanks to Lemma 3.2 and ( 4.50 ), it is easy to get that for $s>2$ , (4.55) $\displaystyle|I_{11}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon\bigl(|[\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\nabla\eta|_{2}+|[\Lambda^{s},\eta]\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}|_{2}\bigr)\bigl(|\Lambda^{s}\eta|_{2}+|\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\Lambda^{s}\boldsymbol{u}|_{2}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\eta|_{H^{s}}(|\eta|_{H^{s}}+\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2})$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}(|\eta|_{H^{s}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2}).$ For $I_{12}$ , integrating by parts, there holds $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I_{12}=&-2\epsilon\sum_{j=1}^{2}([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\epsilon u_{j}\Lambda^{s}\eta+(1+\epsilon\eta)\Lambda^{s}u_{j})_{2}\\
&\quad-2\epsilon^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\nabla\bigl([\Lambda^{s},\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\,|\,\nabla\Lambda^{s}u_{j})_{2}\end{aligned}$ which along with ( 4.50 ) and Lemma 3.2 implies that (4.56) $\displaystyle|I_{12}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigl(\epsilon|\boldsymbol{u}|_{\infty}|\eta|_{H^{s}}+(1+\epsilon|\eta|_{\infty})|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}\bigr)+\epsilon^{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}.$ Thanks to ( 4.55 ) and ( 4.56 ), we get that (4.57) $|I_{1}|\lesssim\epsilon|{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{H^{s}}\bigl(|\eta|_{H^{s}}^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|_{X^{s}_{\epsilon}}^{2}\bigr).$ Estimate for $I_{2}$ .
